```python
def visualize_attention(model, dataset, device, step, out_dir):
    model.eval()
    attention_patterns = []
    
    for _ in range(5):  # Get 5 random examples
        x, y, _ = dataset.fetch_val_example()
        x = torch.tensor(x).unsqueeze(0).to(device)
        
        with torch.no_grad():
            output = model(x)
            # Assuming the last layer's attention is what we want to visualize
            attention = model.model[0].attention_weights.squeeze(0).cpu().numpy()
        
        attention_patterns.append(attention)
    
    # Plot attention patterns
    fig, axes = plt.subplots(1, 5, figsize=(20, 4))
    for i, attn in enumerate(attention_patterns):
        sns.heatmap(attn, ax=axes[i], cmap='viridis')
        axes[i].set_title(f'Example {i+1}')
    
    plt.tight_layout()
    plt.savefig(os.path.join(out_dir, f'attention_pattern_step_{step}.png'))
    plt.close()
=======
def visualize_attention(model, dataset, device, step, out_dir):
    model.eval()
    attention_patterns = []
    inputs = []
    outputs = []
    
    for _ in range(5):  # Get 5 random examples
        x, y, equation = dataset.fetch_val_example()
        x = torch.tensor(x).unsqueeze(0).to(device)
        
        with torch.no_grad():
            output = model(x)
            # Get attention weights from all layers
            attention = [layer.attention_weights.squeeze(0).cpu().numpy() for layer in model.model if hasattr(layer, 'attention_weights')]
        
        attention_patterns.append(attention)
        inputs.append(equation[:-1])  # Input equation without the result
        outputs.append(dataset.idx2vocab[y + 2])  # Convert output index to vocabulary
    
    # Plot attention patterns
    fig, axes = plt.subplots(len(attention_patterns), len(attention[0]), figsize=(20, 4*len(attention_patterns)))
    for i, (attn, inp, out) in enumerate(zip(attention_patterns, inputs, outputs)):
        for j,layer_attn in enumerate(attn):
            sns.heatmap(layer_attn, ax=axes[i,j] if len(attention_patterns) > 1 else axes[j], cmap='viridis')
            axes[i,j].set_title(f'Layer {j+1}, Input: {inp}, Output: {out}' if len(attention_patterns) > 1 else f'Example {i+1}, Layer {j+1}\nInput: {inp}, Output: {out}')
    
    plt.tight_layout()
    plt.savefig(os.path.join(out_dir, f'attention_pattern_step_{step}.png'))
    plt.close()

def analyze_permutation_task(model, dataset, device, out_dir):
    model.eval()
    correct = 0
    total = 0
    attention_patterns = []
    
    for _ in range(100):  # Analyze 100 examples
        x, y, equation = dataset.fetch_val_example()
        x = torch.tensor(x).unsqueeze(0).to(device)
        y = torch.tensor(y).to(device)
        
        with torch.no_grad():
            output = model(x)
            pred = torch.argmax(output[-1, :, :], dim=1)
            correct += (pred == y).sum().item()
            total += 1
            
            # Get attention weights from all layers
            attention = [layer.attention_weights.squeeze(0).cpu().numpy() for layer in model.model if hasattr(layer, 'attention_weights')]
            attention_patterns.append((attention, equation, dataset.idx2vocab[y + 2], dataset.idx2vocab[pred.item() + 2]))
    
    accuracy = correct / total
    
    # Analyze attention patterns
    avg_attention = [np.mean([pattern[0][i] for pattern in attention_patterns], axis=0) for i in range(len(attention_patterns[0][0]))]
    
    # Plot average attention patterns
    fig, axes = plt.subplots(1, len(avg_attention), figsize=(20, 4))
    for i, attn in enumerate(avg_attention):
        sns.heatmap(attn, ax=axes[i], cmap='viridis')
        axes[i].set_title(f'Layer {i+1} Average Attention')
    
    plt.tight_layout()
    plt.savefig(os.path.join(out_dir, 'permutation_avg_attention.png'))
    plt.close()
    
    # Analyze correct vs incorrect predictions
    correct_patterns = [pattern for pattern in attention_patterns if pattern[2] == pattern[3]]
    incorrect_patterns = [pattern for pattern in attention_patterns if pattern[2] != pattern[3]]
    
    with open(os.path.join(out_dir, 'permutation_analysis.txt'), 'w') as f:
        f.write(f"Permutation Task Analysis\n")
        f.write(f"Accuracy: {accuracy:.2f}\n\n")
        f.write(f"Number of correct predictions: {len(correct_patterns)}\n")
        f.write(f"Number of incorrect predictions: {len(incorrect_patterns)}\n\n")
        f.write("Sample of correct predictions:\n")
        for pattern in correct_patterns[:5]:
            f.write(f"Input: {pattern[1]}, True Output: {pattern[2]}, Predicted Output: {pattern[3]}\n")
        f.write("\nSample of incorrect predictions:\n")
        for pattern in incorrect_patterns[:5]:
            f.write(f"Input: {pattern[1]}, True Output: {pattern[2]}, Predicted Output: {pattern[3]}\n")
    
    return accuracy, avg_attention
```

```python
def run(out_dir, dataset, seed_offset):
    os.makedirs(out_dir, exist_ok=True)
    device = torch.device("cuda" if torch.cuda.is_available() else "cpu")
    torch.manual_seed(1337 + seed_offset)
    train_loader, val_loader, n_vocab, n_output = get_data(
        operation=dataset,
        prime=97,
        training_fraction=0.5,
        batch_size=512,
    )

    # Create the dataset object
    dataset_obj = operation_mod_p_data(dataset, 97, 0.5)

    model = Transformer(
        num_layers=2,
        dim_model=128,
        num_heads=4,
        vocab_size=n_vocab,
        output_size=n_output,
        seq_len=5,
    ).to(device)

    optimizer = torch.optim.AdamW(
        model.parameters(),
        lr=1e-3,
        betas=(0.9, 0.98),
        weight_decay=0.5,
    )
    num_train_batches = 10
    num_eval_batches = 8
    num_total_updates = 7500
    warmup_steps = 50
    scheduler = torch.optim.lr_scheduler.LambdaLR(
        optimizer, lr_lambda=lambda s: min(s / warmup_steps, 1)
    )

    final_info, train_log_info, val_log_info = [], [], []
    step_val_acc_99 = num_total_updates
    invariance_scores = []
    max_invariance_score = 0
    invariance_point = num_total_updates
    grokking_point = num_total_updates
    
    # Visualize attention at the start (pre-grokking)
    visualize_attention(model, dataset_obj, device, 0, out_dir)
    
    for ep in range(num_total_updates // num_train_batches):
        train_metrics = train(
            model,
            train_loader,
            optimizer,
            scheduler,
            device,
            num_train_batches,
        )
        val_metrics = evaluate(
            model,
            val_loader,
            device,
            num_eval_batches,
        )
        train_metrics["step"] = (ep + 1) * num_train_batches
        val_metrics["step"] = (ep + 1) * num_train_batches

        if (ep + 1) * num_train_batches % 100 == 0:
            inv_score = invariance_score(model, dataset_obj, device)
            invariance_scores.append({"step": (ep + 1) * num_train_batches, "score": inv_score})
            if inv_score > max_invariance_score:
                max_invariance_score = inv_score
            if invariance_point == num_total_updates and inv_score > 0.9 * max_invariance_score:
                invariance_point = (ep + 1) * num_train_batches
                # Visualize attention at invariance point
                visualize_attention(model, dataset_obj, device, invariance_point, out_dir)

        if step_val_acc_99 == num_total_updates and val_metrics["val_accuracy"] > 0.99:
            step_val_acc_99 = val_metrics["step"]
            grokking_point = step_val_acc_99
            # Visualize attention at grokking point
            visualize_attention(model, dataset_obj, device, grokking_point, out_dir)
        
        train_log_info.append(train_metrics)
        val_log_info.append(val_metrics)

    # Visualize attention at the end (post-grokking)
    visualize_attention(model, dataset_obj, device, num_total_updates, out_dir)

    final_info = {
        "final_train_loss": train_metrics["train_loss"],
        "final_val_loss": val_metrics["val_loss"],
        "final_train_acc": train_metrics["train_accuracy"],
        "final_val_acc": val_metrics["val_accuracy"],
        "step_val_acc_99": step_val_acc_99,
        "invariance_point": invariance_point,
        "grokking_point": grokking_point,
    }
    print(final_info)
    with open(
        os.path.join(out_dir, f"final_info_{dataset}_{seed_offset}.json"), "w"
    ) as f:
        json.dump(final_info, f)
    
    # Save invariance scores
    with open(
        os.path.join(out_dir, f"invariance_scores_{dataset}_{seed_offset}.json"), "w"
    ) as f:
        json.dump(invariance_scores, f)
    
    return final_info, train_log_info, val_log_info, invariance_scores
=======
def run(out_dir, dataset, seed_offset):
    os.makedirs(out_dir, exist_ok=True)
    device = torch.device("cuda" if torch.cuda.is_available() else "cpu")
    torch.manual_seed(1337 + seed_offset)
    train_loader, val_loader, n_vocab, n_output = get_data(
        operation=dataset,
        prime=97,
        training_fraction=0.5,
        batch_size=512,
    )

    # Create the dataset object
    dataset_obj = operation_mod_p_data(dataset, 97, 0.5)

    model = Transformer(
        num_layers=2,
        dim_model=128,
        num_heads=4,
        vocab_size=n_vocab,
        output_size=n_output,
        seq_len=5,
    ).to(device)

    optimizer = torch.optim.AdamW(
        model.parameters(),
        lr=1e-3,
        betas=(0.9, 0.98),
        weight_decay=0.5,
    )
    num_train_batches = 10
    num_eval_batches = 8
    num_total_updates = 7500
    warmup_steps = 50
    scheduler = torch.optim.lr_scheduler.LambdaLR(
        optimizer, lr_lambda=lambda s: min(s / warmup_steps, 1)
    )

    final_info, train_log_info, val_log_info = [], [], []
    step_val_acc_99 = num_total_updates
    invariance_scores = []
    max_invariance_score = 0
    invariance_point = num_total_updates
    grokking_point = num_total_updates
    
    # Visualize attention at the start (pre-grokking)
    visualize_attention(model, dataset_obj, device, 0, out_dir)
    
    for ep in range(num_total_updates // num_train_batches):
        train_metrics = train(
            model,
            train_loader,
            optimizer,
            scheduler,
            device,
            num_train_batches,
        )
        val_metrics = evaluate(
            model,
            val_loader,
            device,
            num_eval_batches,
        )
        train_metrics["step"] = (ep + 1) * num_train_batches
        val_metrics["step"] = (ep + 1) * num_train_batches

        if (ep + 1) * num_train_batches % 100 == 0:
            inv_score = invariance_score(model, dataset_obj, device)
            invariance_scores.append({"step": (ep + 1) * num_train_batches, "score": inv_score})
            if inv_score > max_invariance_score:
                max_invariance_score = inv_score
            if invariance_point == num_total_updates and inv_score > 0.9 * max_invariance_score:
                invariance_point = (ep + 1) * num_train_batches
                # Visualize attention at invariance point
                visualize_attention(model, dataset_obj, device, invariance_point, out_dir)

        if step_val_acc_99 == num_total_updates and val_metrics["val_accuracy"] > 0.99:
            step_val_acc_99 = val_metrics["step"]
            grokking_point = step_val_acc_99
            # Visualize attention at grokking point
            visualize_attention(model, dataset_obj, device, grokking_point, out_dir)
        
        train_log_info.append(train_metrics)
        val_log_info.append(val_metrics)

    # Visualize attention at the end (post-grokking)
    visualize_attention(model, dataset_obj, device, num_total_updates, out_dir)

    final_info = {
        "final_train_loss": train_metrics["train_loss"],
        "final_val_loss": val_metrics["val_loss"],
        "final_train_acc": train_metrics["train_accuracy"],
        "final_val_acc": val_metrics["val_accuracy"],
        "step_val_acc_99": step_val_acc_99,
        "invariance_point": invariance_point,
        "grokking_point": grokking_point,
    }
    
    # Perform detailed analysis for permutation task
    if dataset == "permutation":
        perm_accuracy, avg_attention = analyze_permutation_task(model, dataset_obj, device, out_dir)
        final_info["permutation_analysis"] = {
            "accuracy": perm_accuracy,
            "avg_attention": [attn.tolist() for attn in avg_attention]
        }
    
    print(final_info)
    with open(
        os.path.join(out_dir, f"final_info_{dataset}_{seed_offset}.json"), "w"
    ) as f:
        json.dump(final_info, f)
    
    # Save invariance scores
    with open(
        os.path.join(out_dir, f"invariance_scores_{dataset}_{seed_offset}.json"), "w"
    ) as f:
        json.dump(invariance_scores, f)
    
    return final_info, train_log_info, val_log_info, invariance_scores
>>>>>>> REPLACE
```

experiment.py

    means = {f"{k}_mean": np.mean(v) for k, v in final_info_dict.items()}

=======
    means = {}
    stderrs = {}
    for k, v in final_info_dict.items():
        if isinstance(v[0], dict):
            # Handle nested dictionary
            means[f"{k}_mean"] = {inner_k: np.mean([d[inner_k] for d in v]) for inner_k in v[0].keys()}
            stderrs[f"{k}_stderr"] = {inner_k: np.std([d[inner_k] for d in v]) / len(v) for inner_k in v[0].keys()}
        else:
            # Handle regular list
            means[f"{k}_mean"] = np.mean(v)
            stderrs[f"{k}_stderr"] = np.std(v) / len(v)

## Run 4: Detailed Analysis of Permutation Task
Experiment description: This run focuses on a more detailed analysis of the permutation task, which has consistently shown poor generalization in previous runs.    - The permutation task does not exhibit grokking within the training period.

   a. Increase model capacity (more layers, wider layers) to handle the complexity of permutations.
   b. Implement a curriculum learning strategy that gradually increases the complexity of permutations.

Now, let's add some new plotting functions to generate more relevant plots for  
our final writeup:                                                              

plot.py                                                                         

                                                                                
 <<<<<<< SEARCH                                                                 
     plt.close()                                                                
                                                                                
 # Original plots (keeping these for reference)                                 
 # ... [rest of the original plotting code]                                     
 =======                                                                        
     plt.close()                                                                
                                                                                
 def plot_invariance_vs_accuracy(results_info, datasets, runs, colors, out_dir) 
     for dataset in datasets:                                                   
         plt.figure(figsize=(12, 8))                                            
         for i, run in enumerate(runs):                                         
             if run in results_info and dataset in results_info[run]:           
                 steps = results_info[run][dataset]["step"]                     
                 val_acc = results_info[run][dataset]["val_acc"]                
                 inv_score = results_info[run][dataset]["invariance_score"]     
                                                                                
                 plt.plot(steps, val_acc, label=f'{labels[run]} - Val Acc',     
 color=colors[i], linestyle='-')                                                
                 plt.plot(steps, inv_score, label=f'{labels[run]} - Inv Score', 
 color=colors[i], linestyle='--')                                               
                                                                                
         plt.title(f"Validation Accuracy vs Invariance Score for {dataset}      
 Dataset")                                                                      
         plt.xlabel("Update Steps")                                             
         plt.ylabel("Score")                                                    
         plt.legend()                                                           
         plt.grid(True, which="both", ls="-", alpha=0.2)                        
         plt.tight_layout()                                                     
         plt.savefig(os.path.join(out_dir, f"inv_vs_acc_{dataset}.png"))        
         plt.close()                                                            
                                                                                
 def plot_grokking_points(results_info, datasets, runs, colors, out_dir):       
     grokking_points = {dataset: [] for dataset in datasets}                    
     invariance_points = {dataset: [] for dataset in datasets}                  
                                                                                
     for run in runs:                                                           
         if run in results_info:                                                
             for dataset in datasets:                                           
                 if dataset in results_info[run]:                               
                     grokking_points[dataset].append(results_info[run][dataset] 
 et("grokking_point", 7500))                                                    
                     invariance_points[dataset].append(results_info[run][datase 
 .get("invariance_point", 7500))                                                
                                                                                
     x = np.arange(len(datasets))                                               
     width = 0.35                                                               
                                                                                
     fig, ax = plt.subplots(figsize=(12, 8))                                    
     rects1 = ax.bar(x - width/2, [np.mean(grokking_points[d]) for d in         
 datasets], width, label='Grokking Point', color='blue', alpha=0.7)             
     rects2 = ax.bar(x + width/2, [np.mean(invariance_points[d]) for d in       
 datasets], width, label='Invariance Point', color='red', alpha=0.7)            
                                                                                
     ax.set_ylabel('Steps')                                                     
     ax.set_title('Grokking Point vs Invariance Point')                         
     ax.set_xticks(x)                                                           
     ax.set_xticklabels(datasets)                                               
     ax.legend()                                                                
                                                                                
     fig.tight_layout()                                                         
     plt.savefig(os.path.join(out_dir, "grokking_vs_invariance_points.png"))    
     plt.close()                                                                
                                                                                
 # Call the new plotting functions                                              
 plot_invariance_vs_accuracy(results_info, datasets, runs, colors, ".")         
 plot_grokking_points(results_info, datasets, runs, colors, ".")                
                                                                                
 # Original plots (keeping these for reference)                                 
 # ... [rest of the original plotting code]                                     
 =======

 2 Add two new plotting functions:                                              
    • plot_invariance_vs_accuracy: This function will plot the validation       
      accuracy and invariance score on the same graph for each dataset, allowing
      us to visualize how these metrics evolve together during training.        
    • plot_grokking_points: This function will create a bar plot comparing the  
      grokking points and invariance points across different datasets and runs, 
      providing a clear visualization of when these key events occur in the     
      training process.                                                         
 3 Call these new plotting functions after the existing plots are generated.

datasets and model configurations.

 def plot_invariance_vs_accuracy(results_info, datasets, runs, colors, out_dir) 
     for dataset in datasets:                                                   
         plt.figure(figsize=(12, 8))                                            
         for i, run in enumerate(runs):                                         
             if run in results_info and dataset in results_info[run]:           
                 steps = results_info[run][dataset]["step"]                     
                 val_acc = results_info[run][dataset]["val_acc"]                
                 inv_score = results_info[run][dataset].get("invariance_score", 
 [])                                                                            
                                                                                
                 plt.plot(steps, val_acc, label=f'{labels[run]} - Val Acc',     
 color=colors[i], linestyle='-')                                                
                                                                                
                 if len(inv_score) == len(steps):                               
                     plt.plot(steps, inv_score, label=f'{labels[run]} - Inv     
 Score', color=colors[i], linestyle='--')                                       
                 else:                                                          
                     print(f"Warning: Invariance score data for {dataset} in    
 {run} has incorrect length. Skipping this plot.")                              
                                                                                
         plt.title(f"Validation Accuracy vs Invariance Score for {dataset}      
 Dataset")                                                                      
         plt.xlabel("Update Steps")                                             
         plt.ylabel("Score")                                                    
         plt.legend()                                                           
         plt.grid(True, which="both", ls="-", alpha=0.2)                        
         plt.tight_layout()                                                     
         plt.savefig(os.path.join(out_dir, f"inv_vs_acc_{dataset}.png"))        
         plt.close()

 only 0.85% on the full validation set. This could involve:                     
    a. Examining the distribution of permutations in the training and validatio 
 sets.                                                                          
    b. Analyzing if the model is biased towards certain types of permutations.  
 3. Experiment with modifications specifically targeted at improving permutatio 
 task performance:                                                              
    a. Increase model capacity (more layers, wider layers) to handle the        
 complexity of permutations.                                                    
    b. Implement a curriculum learning strategy that gradually increases the    
 complexity of permutations.                                                    
    c. Explore alternative attention mechanisms or positional encodings that    
 might be more suitable for learning permutations.                              
 4. Conduct a longer training run for the permutation task to see if grokking   
 occurs at a later point.                                                       
 5. Implement additional metrics to track the model's understanding of          
 permutation properties (e.g., associativity, inverse operations) throughout

 Plot Descriptions:                                                             
                                                                                
 1. combined_metrics_{dataset}.png                                              
    This plot shows the evolution of three key metrics over the course of       
 training for each dataset (x_div_y, x_minus_y, x_plus_y, permutation):         
    - Training Loss: Represented by solid lines, this metric shows how well the 
 model is fitting the training data.                                            
    - Validation Accuracy: Represented by dashed lines, this metric indicates t 
 model's performance on unseen data.                                            
    - Invariance Score: Represented by dotted lines (only for run_1), this metr 
 measures how consistent the model's outputs are under task-specific            
 transformations.                                                               
    The x-axis represents the number of update steps, while the y-axis shows th 
 value of each metric. Different colors represent different runs. This plot is  
 crucial for identifying the grokking phenomenon, characterized by a sudden     
 improvement in validation accuracy after a period of stagnation.               
                                                                                
 2. inv_vs_acc_{dataset}.png                                                    
    This plot directly compares the Validation Accuracy and Invariance Score fo 
 each dataset:                                                                  
    - Validation Accuracy: Solid lines show the model's performance on unseen   
 data.                                                                          
    - Invariance Score: Dashed lines represent the model's consistency under    
 task-specific transformations.                                                 
    The x-axis shows the number of update steps, and the y-axis represents the  
 score (0-1 range for both metrics). This visualization helps in understanding  
 the relationship between the model's generalization ability and its learning o 
 task-specific invariances.                                                     
                                                                                
 3. grokking_vs_invariance_points.png                                           
    This bar plot compares two critical points in the learning process across a 
 datasets:                                                                      
    - Grokking Point: The blue bars represent the step at which the validation  
 accuracy exceeds 95%.                                                          
    - Invariance Point: The red bars show the step at which the invariance scor 
 exceeds 90% of its maximum value.                                              
    The x-axis lists the datasets, and the y-axis shows the number of steps. Th 
 plot helps in analyzing whether the learning of invariances precedes, coincide 
 with, or follows the grokking phenomenon across different tasks.               
                                                                                
 4. attention_pattern_step_{step}.png                                           
    These plots visualize the attention patterns of the model at key points     
 during training (pre-grokking, invariance point, grokking point, post-grokking 
 for each dataset. Each plot contains multiple heatmaps, one for each attention 
 layer in the model. The x and y axes of each heatmap represent the input       
 sequence positions, with darker colors indicating stronger attention. These    
 visualizations help in understanding how the model's focus on different parts  
 the input changes as it learns the task and achieves invariance.               
                                                                                
 5. permutation_avg_attention.png                                               
    This plot shows the average attention patterns across all layers for the    
 permutation task. It consists of multiple heatmaps, one for each attention     
 layer, averaged over many examples. The x and y axes represent the input       
 sequence positions, with color intensity indicating the strength of attention. 
 This visualization helps in identifying any consistent patterns in how the mod 
 attends to different parts of the permutation input.                           
                                                                                
 These plots collectively provide a comprehensive view of the learning dynamics 
 the relationship between invariance and generalization, and the internal       
 attention mechanisms of the model across different tasks. They are crucial for 
 understanding the grokking phenomenon and the role of invariance in the model' 
 learning process.

Plot Descriptions:

1. combined_metrics_{dataset}.png
   This plot shows the evolution of three key metrics over the course of 
training for each dataset (x_div_y, x_minus_y, x_plus_y, permutation):
   - Training Loss: Represented by solid lines, this metric shows how well the 
model is fitting the training data.
   - Validation Accuracy: Represented by dashed lines, this metric indicates the
model's performance on unseen data.
   - Invariance Score: Represented by dotted lines (only for run_1), this metric
measures how consistent the model's outputs are under task-specific 
transformations.
   The x-axis represents the number of update steps, while the y-axis shows the 
value of each metric. Different colors represent different runs. This plot is 
crucial for identifying the grokking phenomenon, characterized by a sudden 
improvement in validation accuracy after a period of stagnation.

2. inv_vs_acc_{dataset}.png
   This plot directly compares the Validation Accuracy and Invariance Score for 
each dataset:
   - Validation Accuracy: Solid lines show the model's performance on unseen 
data.
   - Invariance Score: Dashed lines represent the model's consistency under 
task-specific transformations.
   The x-axis shows the number of update steps, and the y-axis represents the 
score (0-1 range for both metrics). This visualization helps in understanding 
the relationship between the model's generalization ability and its learning of 
task-specific invariances.

3. grokking_vs_invariance_points.png
   This bar plot compares two critical points in the learning process across all
datasets:
   - Grokking Point: The blue bars represent the step at which the validation 
accuracy exceeds 95%.
   - Invariance Point: The red bars show the step at which the invariance score 
exceeds 90% of its maximum value.
   The x-axis lists the datasets, and the y-axis shows the number of steps. This
plot helps in analyzing whether the learning of invariances precedes, coincides 
with, or follows the grokking phenomenon across different tasks.

4. attention_pattern_step_{step}.png
   These plots visualize the attention patterns of the model at key points 
during training (pre-grokking, invariance point, grokking point, post-grokking) 
for each dataset. Each plot contains multiple heatmaps, one for each attention 
layer in the model. The x and y axes of each heatmap represent the input 
sequence positions, with darker colors indicating stronger attention. These 
visualizations help in understanding how the model's focus on different parts of
the input changes as it learns the task and achieves invariance.

5. permutation_avg_attention.png
   This plot shows the average attention patterns across all layers for the 
permutation task. It consists of multiple heatmaps, one for each attention 
layer, averaged over many examples. The x and y axes represent the input 
sequence positions, with color intensity indicating the strength of attention. 
This visualization helps in identifying any consistent patterns in how the model
attends to different parts of the permutation input.

These plots collectively provide a comprehensive view of the learning dynamics, 
the relationship between invariance and generalization, and the internal 
attention mechanisms of the model across different tasks. They are crucial for 
understanding the grokking phenomenon and the role of invariance in the model's 
learning process.

    - Grokking Point: The blue bars represent the step at which the validation  
 accuracy exceeds 95%.                                                          
    - Invariance Point: The red bars show the step at which the invariance scor 
 exceeds 90% of its maximum value.                                              
    The x-axis lists the datasets, and the y-axis shows the number of steps. Th 
 plot helps in analyzing whether the learning of invariances precedes, coincide 
 with, or follows the grokking phenomenon across different tasks.               
                                                                                
 4. attention_pattern_step_{step}.png                                           
    These plots visualize the attention patterns of the model at key points     
 during training (pre-grokking, invariance point, grokking point, post-grokking 
 for each dataset. Each plot contains multiple heatmaps, one for each attention 
 layer in the model. The x and y axes of each heatmap represent the input       
 sequence positions, with darker colors indicating stronger attention. These    
 visualizations help in understanding how the model's focus on different parts  
 the input changes as it learns the task and achieves invariance.               
                                                                                
 5. permutation_avg_attention.png                                               
    This plot shows the average attention patterns across all layers for the    
 permutation task. It consists of multiple heatmaps, one for each attention     
 layer, averaged over many examples. The x and y axes represent the input       
 sequence positions, with color intensity indicating the strength of attention. 
 This visualization helps in identifying any consistent patterns in how the mod 
 attends to different parts of the permutation input.                           
                                                                                
 These plots collectively provide a comprehensive view of the learning dynamics 
 the relationship between invariance and generalization, and the internal       
 attention mechanisms of the model across different tasks. They are crucial for 
 understanding the grokking phenomenon and the role of invariance in the model' 
 learning process.
